=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a 2D integer matrix grid of size n x m, where each element is either 0, 1, or 2.
A V-shaped diagonal segment is defined as:

The segment starts with 1.
The subsequent elements follow this infinite sequence: 2, 0, 2, 0, ....
The segment:
	
Starts along a diagonal direction (top-left to bottom-right, bottom-right to top-left, top-right to bottom-left, or bottom-left to top-right).
Continues the sequence in the same diagonal direction.
Makes at most one clockwise 90-degree turn to another diagonal direction while maintaining the sequence.




Return the length of the longest V-shaped diagonal segment. If no valid segment exists, return 0.
 
Example 1:

Input: grid = [[2,2,1,2,2],[2,0,2,2,0],[2,0,1,1,0],[1,0,2,2,2],[2,0,0,2,2]]
Output: 5
Explanation:

The longest V-shaped diagonal segment has a length of 5 and follows these coordinates: (0,2) → (1,3) → (2,4), takes a 90-degree clockwise turn at (2,4), and continues as (3,3) → (4,2).

Example 2:

Input: grid = [[2,2,2,2,2],[2,0,2,2,0],[2,0,1,1,0],[1,0,2,2,2],[2,0,0,2,2]]
Output: 4
Explanation:

The longest V-shaped diagonal segment has a length of 4 and follows these coordinates: (2,3) → (3,2), takes a 90-degree clockwise turn at (3,2), and continues as (2,1) → (1,0).

Example 3:

Input: grid = [[1,2,2,2,2],[2,2,2,2,0],[2,0,0,0,0],[0,0,2,2,2],[2,0,0,2,0]]
Output: 5
Explanation:

The longest V-shaped diagonal segment has a length of 5 and follows these coordinates: (0,0) → (1,1) → (2,2) → (3,3) → (4,4).

Example 4:

Input: grid = [[1]]
Output: 1
Explanation:
The longest V-shaped diagonal segment has a length of 1 and follows these coordinates: (0,0).

 
Constraints:

n == grid.length
m == grid[i].length
1 <= n, m <= 500
grid[i][j] is either 0, 1 or 2.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def lenOfVDiagonal(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Then:
```
@lru_cache(None)
def dp(i,j,d,turned):
    best = 1  # current cell
    # try continue straight
    ni,nj = i+dirs[d][0], j+dirs[d][1]
    if in bounds and grid[ni][nj] == next_val(grid[i][j]):
        best = max(best, 1 + dp(ni,nj,d,turned))
    # try turn clockwise if not turned
    if turned == 0:
        nd = (d+1)%4
        ni,nj = i+dirs[nd][0], j+dirs[nd][1]
        if in bounds and grid[ni][nj] == next_val(grid[i][j]):
            best = max(best, 1 + dp(ni,nj,nd,1))
    return best
```
Then answer = max dp(i,j,d,0) over all cells with grid[i][j]==1 and d=0..3? But if we allow turn at start, dp(i,j,d,0) includes turning immediately to d+1. But max over d covers all. However, if we call dp for start with all d, and dp uses memoization, it may compute states for cells with value 2/0 as current. Is that okay? Yes.

But wait: The recurrence as written returns max length of a continuation starting at current cell in direction d, with `turned` indicating whether a turn has already been used before arriving at current cell? Let's define `turned` = number of turns already made along the path up to and including at current cell? In recurrence, if turned==0, we can turn when leaving current cell. If turned==1, no more turns. At initial call turned=0, no turn used yet. If we turn at current cell, we set turned=1 for next state. That means the turn is counted as used at current cell. Good.

But there is a potential issue: If we call dp(i,j,d,0) on a cell with value 1 and allow turn immediately, then the path has made a turn at the starting cell before moving in direction d. In the recurrence, when at start and turned=0, we try straight in d and turn to d+1. If turn to d+1 and move, the next state has turned=1. This path's first actual movement is in direction d+1, not d. It is equivalent to starting with direction d+1 and turned=0, which can later turn again. But because the turned-at-start state is turned=1, it cannot turn later. So it might be shorter than the equivalent straight-start path. But max over all d includes the equivalent straight-start path with turned=0, so okay. But could this turned-at-start path be the only way to achieve a path that is invalid? It is valid as straight d+1 no-turn, so no issue. It doesn't create a path with two turns because turned=1 prevents further turns. So safe.

But wait: If we call dp(i,j,d,0) for a start, and it turns immediately to d+1, then later cannot turn. But what if the path should have initial direction d, then turn at the first cell? That's impossible because no movement in d. So no valid path lost. Good.

However, there is another subtlety: The recurrence tries turn at every cell with turned==0, including cells that are not the original start but maybe after moving straight some steps. That's allowed: the segment can make a turn at any cell after at least one move? If the current cell is reached by moving in direction d, then turning to d+1 at current cell is a turn after at least one move, valid. If current cell is start and no move, turning is redundant but valid as straight. So okay.

Maybe there is an even simpler DP with 2 directions? Let's explore.

Because allowed turn is always clockwise, the path consists of two straight diagonal segments where the second direction is clockwise from first. There are four possible turn pairs:
0 DR -> 1 DL
1 DL -> 2 UL
2 UL -> 3 UR
3 UR -> 0 DR
Or no turn: any direction.

We can compute for each cell the maximum length of a straight valid segment starting at that cell in each direction, following value sequence. Then combine with turn.

Let's define `straight[i][j][d]` = maximum number of cells in a valid sequence starting at (i,j) and moving in direction d, with no turns, assuming (i,j) is a valid current sequence cell (value matches sequence). This can be computed by DP backwards:
```
straight[i][j][d] = 1 + straight[ni][nj][d] if neighbor value == next_val(grid[i][j]) else 1
```
Because straight continuation depends only on current cell value and direction. This is simple and no turn state. Then a V-shaped segment with at most one turn can be seen as:
- Start at cell s with value1, initial direction d0.
- Move straight along d0 for some steps to a turn cell t (could be s if no move? but turn at start redundant), then turn clockwise to d1=(d0+1)%4, then move straight along d1 for some steps.
The total length = (length of straight segment from s in d0 up to t) + (length of straight segment from t in d1) - 1? Need avoid double count t. More generally, if we choose a turn cell t and initial direction d0, the segment starts at some cell s that lies on the straight diagonal line in direction d0 backwards from t, and s must be a 1. Then the first part is the straight valid segment from s to t along d0. The second part is straight valid segment from t in d1. The total length = (number of cells from s to t inclusive) + (number of cells from t in d1 excluding t?) Wait `straight[t][d1]` includes t and subsequent cells in d1. If we concatenate, t counted once. So total = first_len + second_len - 1, where first_len = distance from s to t +1, second_len = straight[t][d1]. But first_len must be exactly the straight valid segment from s in d0 that reaches t. Since straight[s][d0] might continue beyond t; but we can choose to turn at t, so first part length can be any prefix of straight[s][d0] up to t. For a fixed t and d0, we need find nearest? Actually s must be a 1 and the values from s to t along d0 must match sequence. Since sequence values alternate, there may be multiple possible s along the same diagonal line that are 1 and valid up to t. We need maximize total length.     straight_len[i][j][d] = 1 + straight_len[neighbor][d]
else:
    straight_len[i][j][d] = 1
```
This can be computed by iterating cells in reverse order along each direction. For d=DR (1,1), process i from n-1 down to 0, j from m-1 down to 0? Since neighbor (i+1,j+1) has larger indices, yes. For d=UR (-1,1), neighbor (i-1,j+1), process i from 0..n-1, j from m-1 down to 0. Or use recursion. But we can compute with loops.

ans = max(ans, pre_len[i][j][d] + straight_len[i][j][cw(d)] - 1)

- If grid[i][j] == 1: pre_len[i][j][d] = 1 for all d? Actually a segment of length 1 can be considered as ending at start with any initial direction? It hasn't moved yet. - For every cell (i,j) and direction d, if pre_len[i][j][d] > 0, then a segment with no turn? Actually `pre_len[i][j][d]` is just the length of the current valid run ending at (i,j) along direction d, starting from a 1 and following value sequence. We can compute it by scanning diagonals in reverse? Let's define for each direction d, the predecessor p = (i - di, j - dj). Then:
```
if grid[i][j] == 1:
    pre_len[i][j][d] = 1
elif p in bounds and grid[i][j] == next_val(grid[p]) and pre_len[p][d] > 0:
    pre_len[i][j][d] = pre_len[p][d] + 1
else:
    pre_len[i][j][d] = 0
```
This is a simple DP along each diagonal. We can compute for all d by iterating cells in an order such that p is processed before i. For d=DR (1,1), p=(i-1,j-1), so process i increasing, j increasing. For d=DL (1,-1), p=(i-1,j+1), process i increasing, j decreasing? Wait direction vector d=(1,-1), predecessor is (-1,+1), so p=(i-1,j+1). Need process i increasing, j decreasing to have p processed. For d=UL (-1,-1), p=(i+1,j+1), process i decreasing, j decreasing. For d=UR (-1,1), p=(i+1,j-1), process i decreasing, j increasing. Or we can use recursion/memo for simplicity.

`straight_len[i][j][d]` can also be computed by scanning in opposite order:
```
if neighbor in bounds and grid[neighbor] == next_val(grid[i][j]):
    straight_len[i][j][d] = 1 + straight_len[neighbor][d]
else:
    straight_len[i][j][d] = 1
```
This is similar to pre_len but starts from any valid current cell and goes forward. Actually `straight_len` is the length of the maximal valid run starting at (i,j) in direction d, regardless of whether (i,j) is a 1. It can be computed by scanning from the end of each diagonal backward. It is analogous to "remaining length" after turn.

Let's further analyze correctness of pre/straight DP.

Definitions:
- `straight[i][j][d]`: maximum number of cells in a valid diagonal segment that starts at (i,j) and moves in direction d with no turns, including (i,j). It is valid if the sequence of values from (i,j) onward follows the required sequence starting at (i,j)'s value? Wait "starting at (i,j)" in the remaining segment, not necessarily original start. The required sequence from (i,j) onward depends on the value at (i,j). Since values alternate, if (i,j) is value 2, next must be 0; if value0, next 2; if value1, next2. So recurrence using `next_val(grid[i][j])` is correct. It doesn't require knowing absolute position because the value at (i,j) already encodes the parity. But is it possible that a cell with value2 could be at an even position in some invalid sequence? In a valid sequence, value2 only at odd positions. If we start a straight segment at a value2, we are treating it as an odd position. That's the only way to continue with 0. If a predecessor with value0 moves into it, that's consistent (0 at even -> next 2). If a predecessor with value1 moves into it, consistent. So fine.
- `pre[i][j][d]`: maximum number of cells in a valid segment that starts at some 1, has no turns, and ends at (i,j), with the last move direction d (or if length1, direction d is arbitrary). It is valid if values from start to (i,j) follow sequence. Recurrence:
  - If grid[i][j] == 1: pre=1 for all d. This represents a segment consisting solely of this 1. Is it okay to set pre=1 for all d even though no move direction? For combining with turn, it may produce straight no-turn segments as noted. For computing future pre, if we set pre[start][d]=1 for all d, then from start we can move in any direction d to a neighbor if neighbor value=2, giving pre[neighbor][d]=2. This corresponds to a segment starting at 1 and moving in direction d. That's correct. If we only set pre for one direction, we'd need know initial direction. But setting all d works because the first move direction can be any d; the "d" in pre at the start is just the direction that will be used for the first move (or arbitrary if no move). For a length1 segment, all d are possible as potential first move direction. So okay.
  - For non-1: if predecessor p in direction d exists and `grid[i][j] == next_val(grid[p])` and `pre[p][d] > 0`, then `pre[i][j][d] = pre[p][d] + 1`. This extends a no-turn segment ending at p with direction d to i. Note: We don't require p's value? pre[p][d]>0 ensures p is a valid segment ending. Good.
  - Else pre=0 (cannot be the end of a no-turn segment starting from 1 in direction d). But wait, what if grid[i][j] is 1 and also can be reached from a predecessor? The sequence never has 1 after start, so no predecessor should move into a 1 because next_val(0)=2, next_val(2)=0, next_val(1)=2. So grid[i][j]==1 only start. If somehow predecessor value? next_val never 1. So no conflict.
This pre DP computes the longest no-turn segment ending at each cell/direction. Is it possible that a shorter pre at predecessor with different parity could extend while longer pre cannot? We earlier argued parity determined by cell value. Let's verify with pre recurrence. Suppose pre[p][d] >0. The value at p determines next expected. If grid[i][j] == next_val(grid[p]), then any valid segment ending at p can extend to i, regardless of its length, because all such segments have p at a sequence position consistent with grid[p]'s value. But could there be a valid segment ending at p with grid[p]=2 but length even? No, grid[p]=2 implies length odd. So all valid segments ending at p have same parity. Thus pre[p][d] max length dominates shorter. Good.
- Combining: For a one-turn segment, let t be the turn cell, d initial direction, d2=cw(d). The first part from start to t is a no-turn segment ending at t with direction d (if length>1; if length1, direction arbitrary). Its length is at most pre[t][d]. The second part from t onward in direction d2 is a no-turn segment starting at t with direction d2. Its maximum length is straight[t][d2]. The concatenated length is pre[t][d] + straight[t][d2] - 1. This is valid because the value at t is counted once. The first part's last value is grid[t]; the second part's first value is also grid[t], and the sequence continues from grid[t] to next expected. `straight[t][d2]` includes grid[t] and subsequent values following next_val(grid[t]) etc. So concatenation respects sequence. The turn is clockwise from d to d2. Good.
- What about a segment with no turn: It is a no-turn segment starting at 1 and ending at some cell, length pre[end][d]. Taking max over all pre covers all straight segments. Also combining with pre at start and straight at start covers straight segments, but max pre enough. We can initialize ans with max pre. Or answer = max(max pre, max combine). Need include length1 if grid has 1; pre[start][d]=1 so max pre >=1. If no 1, pre all 0, answer 0. Good.

Now, can a valid segment with one turn be represented by choosing t as the turn cell and d as the direction before turn? Yes. The first part may have length 1 (start=t) if the segment turns at the starting cell? But as discussed, that's not a real turn; however, if a valid segment has one turn, the turn cell is not the start because you need move in initial direction before turning. But our combination with pre[t][d]=1 at start and straight[t][d2] gives a straight segment starting in d2, not a one-turn segment. It doesn't hurt. For actual one-turn segments, first part length >=2, and pre[t][d] captures it. Good.

Now, need compute `straight` and `pre` efficiently.

Let's define directions in clockwise order:
```
dirs = [(1,1), (1,-1), (-1,-1), (-1,1)]
```
Indices 0:DR, 1:DL, 2:UL, 3:UR. cw(d) = (d+1)%4.

We need compute `straight` for each d. We can store as 3D list maybe `straight = [[[1]*4 for _ in range(m)] for _ in range(n)]`? That's n lists of m lists of 4 ints; overhead huge but okay? 500*500*4 = 1M ints; list overhead maybe ~8MB for pointers plus ints. But nested lists overhead maybe significant but still maybe okay. We can optimize with flat arrays? Let's consider final code readability vs performance.

Option: Use 4 separate 2D lists, one per direction: `straight = [[[1]*m for _ in range(n)] for _ in range(4)]`? Actually 4 x n x m. Let's design with one pre matrix reused:
- Compute `straight`: 4 x n x m matrices.
- Initialize ans=0.
- For each direction d in 0..3:
  - Create `pre = [[0]*m for _ in range(n)]` (or maybe use a 2D list).
  - Fill pre for direction d in topological order:
    For each cell (i,j) in appropriate order:
      if grid[i][j] == 1:
         pre[i][j] = 1
      else:
         p = (i - di, j - dj)  # predecessor along direction d
         if in bounds and grid[i][j] == nxt(grid[p_i][p_j]) and pre[p_i][p_j] > 0:
             pre[i][j] = pre[p_i][p_j] + 1
         else:
             pre[i][j] = 0
      # update ans
      if pre[i][j] > 0:
          ans = max(ans, pre[i][j])
          d2 = (d+1)%4
          # second part from current cell in direction d2
          second = straight[d2][i][j]
          ans = max(ans, pre[i][j] + second - 1)
  - After direction d done, discard pre.

Valid as straight. Good.

This corresponds to starting at 1 and moving in direction d. Good. If we set pre=1 for all d, then from a 1 we can move in any direction d, as desired. So yes.

- d=DL (1,-1) -> d2=UL (-1,-1). First: row increases, col decreases. Second: row decreases, col decreases. Col only decreases in both, so second cols <= turn col, first cols >= turn col? First before turn has col > turn col (if moving DL, col decreases; before turn col = turn_col + k, so > turn_col). Second after turn has col < turn_col. They only share at turn col. No overlap.
- d=UL (-1,-1) -> d2=UR (-1,1). First: row decreases, col decreases. Second: row decreases, col increases. Row only decreases, so no overlap.
- d=UR (-1,1) -> d2=DR (1,1). First: row decreases, col increases. Second: row increases, col increases. Col only increases, so no overlap.
Thus no repeated cells. Good.

Let's formally prove pre/straight.

Define `straight[d][i][j]`: length of longest valid diagonal segment that starts at (i,j) and continues in direction d without turns. It is valid if the sequence of values starting at (i,j) follows the required alternating sequence from that point. Since the required next value depends only on current value, recurrence holds.

Define `pre[d][i][j]`: length of longest valid segment that starts at some 1 and ends at (i,j), with no turns and with the last move direction d (if length=1, d is arbitrary). Recurrence:
- If grid[i][j]=1, length1.
- Else, any such segment of length>1 must have previous cell p = (i-di, j-dj) and the segment ending at p with same direction d must be valid; and grid[i][j] must be the required next value after grid[p]. Thus pre = pre[p]+1 if condition, else no such segment. This is correct.

Any valid V segment:
- If no turn: it is a no-turn segment starting at 1, so length <= max pre.
- If one turn at cell t from direction d to cw(d): prefix ending at t is a no-turn segment starting at 1 with direction d, length <= pre[d][t]; suffix starting at t in direction cw(d) has length <= straight[cw(d)][t]. Concatenated length <= pre+straight-1. Conversely, taking the prefix achieving pre and suffix achieving straight and concatenating at t gives a valid V segment (provided prefix and suffix values align at t; they do because both include t and suffix follows next value from t). Need ensure prefix length maybe 1 at a 1 and suffix straight from t; then concatenation is straight, valid. So max over t,d of pre+straight-1 covers all one-turn segments and no-turn segments (when prefix length1? Actually no-turn segments also covered by pre alone). Thus answer.

Potential issue: For a one-turn segment, the prefix ending at t must have last move direction d. If prefix length=1 (start=t), there is no last move direction; but a real one-turn segment cannot have prefix length1. Our combination with pre=1 at start and straight[d2] gives a straight segment, not a one-turn segment. But since we also take max pre, no problem. For real one-turn prefix length>=2, pre[d][t] captures it. Good.

Now, let's think about if `pre[d][t]` might be achieved by a prefix that starts at a 1 and has already "turned" before t? No, pre recurrence only extends same direction d from predecessor with same direction d. It never changes direction. So no turn. Good.

Now, let's think about code style.

Potential code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def lenOfVDiagonal(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(grid)
        m = len(grid[0])
        dirs = [(1,1), (1,-1), (-1,-1), (-1,1)]
        
        def nxt(v):
            return 2 if v != 2 else 0
        
        # straight[d][i][j]
        straight = [[[1] * m for _ in range(n)] for _ in range(4)]
        
        # d=0: DR
        for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
            row = straight[0][i]
            for j in range(m-1, -1, -1):
                if i+1 < n and j+1 < m and grid[i+1][j+1] == nxt(grid[i][j]):
                    row[j] = straight[0][i+1][j+1] + 1
        # d=1: DL
        for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
            row = straight[1][i]
            for j in range(m):
                if i+1 < n and j-1 >= 0 and grid[i+1][j-1] == nxt(grid[i][j]):
                    row[j] = straight[1][i+1][j-1] + 1
        # d=2: UL
        for i in range(n):
            row = straight[2][i]
            for j in range(m):
                if i-1 >= 0 and j-1 >= 0 and grid[i-1][j-1] == nxt(grid[i][j]):
                    row[j] = straight[2][i-1][j-1] + 1
        # d=3: UR
        for i in range(n):
            row = straight[3][i]
            for j in range(m-1, -1, -1):
                if i-1 >= 0 and j+1 < m and grid[i-1][j+1] == nxt(grid[i][j]):
                    row[j] = straight[3][i-1][j+1] + 1
        
        ans = 0
        for d in range(4):
            pre = [[0] * m for _ in range(n)]
            di, dj = dirs[d]
            # choose loop order based on d
            if d == 0:
                r_iter = range(n)
                c_iter = lambda: range(m)
            elif d == 1:
                r_iter = range(n)
                c_iter = lambda: range(m-1, -1, -1)
            elif d == 2:
                r_iter = range(n-1, -1, -1)
                c_iter = lambda: range(m-1, -1, -1)
            else: # d==3
                r_iter = range(n-1, -1, -1)
                c_iter = lambda: range(m)
            d2 = (d + 1) % 4
            straight2 = straight[d2]
            for i in r_iter:
                pre_row = pre[i]
                grid_row = grid[i]
                for j in c_iter():
                    if grid_row[j] == 1:
                        pre_row[j] = 1
                    else:
                        pi = i - di
                        pj = j - dj
                        if 0 <= pi < n and 0 <= pj < m:
                            pv = grid[pi][pj]
                            if grid_row[j] == nxt(pv) and pre[pi][pj] > 0:
                                pre_row[j] = pre[pi][pj] + 1
                        # else remains 0
                    if pre_row[j] > 0:
                        if pre_row[j] > ans:
                            ans = pre_row[j]
                        val = pre_row[j] + straight2[i][j] - 1
                        if val > ans:
                            ans = val
        return ans
```
Need check loop order for pre d=2 and d=3. We set:
d=0 DR: predecessor (i-1,j-1), process i increasing, j increasing. r_iter=range(n), c_iter=range(m). Good.
d=1 DL: predecessor (i-1,j+1), process i increasing, j decreasing. r_iter=range(n), c_iter=range(m-1,-1,-1). Good.
d=2 UL: predecessor (i+1,j+1), process i decreasing, j decreasing. r_iter=range(n-1,-1,-1), c_iter=range(m-1,-1,-1). Good.
d=3 UR: predecessor (i+1,j-1), process i decreasing, j increasing. r_iter=range(n-1,-1,-1), c_iter=range(m). Good.

But wait, for d=2 UL, direction vector (-1,-1), predecessor is (i+1,j+1). If process i decreasing, j decreasing, at current (i,j), predecessor (i+1,j+1) has larger i and larger j. Outer i decreasing means i+1 processed before i. Inner j decreasing means j+1 processed before j in same row. Good.
For d=3 UR, direction vector (-1,1), predecessor (i+1,j-1). Process i decreasing, j increasing. At current (i,j), predecessor has larger i, smaller j. Outer i decreasing means i+1 processed before i. Inner j increasing means j-1 processed before j. Good.

Need ensure `pre[pi][pj]` is from the current direction d's pre matrix. Since we reuse `pre` and fill for current d, and `pi,pj` processed earlier due to order. Good.

Now, one subtle bug: In pre recurrence, if grid[i][j] == 1, we set pre=1. But what if the cell is 1 and also could be reached from a predecessor? We argued no. But if grid[i][j]==1, setting pre=1 for all d means we ignore any potential predecessor. Since no predecessor can move into 1, okay. But what if `grid[i][j]==1` and `pre[pi][pj]` from same direction is >0 and `grid[i][j] == nxt(pv)`? `nxt(pv)` is 2 if pv !=2 else 0, never 1. So condition false. Thus no issue.

Now, another subtle bug: In straight computation, we initialize all straight to 1. But for cells with value? straight length at least1 always, because a single cell is a valid segment if it is considered as current position? Wait, `straight` is used as second part after a turn. If the turn cell has value2 or0, a single cell is a valid continuation of length1 (just the turn cell). If the turn cell has value1, a single cell is valid as start. So yes straight at least1 for every cell. But is a single cell with value2 or0 a valid "segment" by itself? The overall segment must start with1, but `straight` is not an overall segment; it's a suffix. A suffix consisting of a single cell is valid as part of a segment if the preceding cell's value matches. So length1 is fine. Good.

Now, let's think about if `straight` for a cell with value2 should be 1 even if no valid predecessor? It's a suffix, not a standalone segment. It can always serve as the turn cell itself, length1. So yes.

Now, let's think about if `straight` recurrence should require `straight[neighbor]` maybe 0 if neighbor value matches but neighbor cannot be part of a valid segment because no start? But suffix validity doesn't require a start; it just needs to follow sequence from current cell. Any cell can be the current position if its value is consistent with some sequence position. All values 0,1,2 are consistent with some position (1 at start, 2 at odd t, 0 at even t). So length1 always valid. Good.

Now, let's think about if a cell value1 could be a suffix after a turn? No, but straight includes it; combination only uses straight at turn cell. If turn cell value1, it's start, so suffix is actually whole segment. Valid.

Now, let's consider if there is a valid segment that starts with 1 and then moves in direction d, but the first move direction d is not the same as the direction stored in pre at the start? We set pre[start][d]=1 for all d, so extending to neighbor in direction d gives pre[neighbor][d]=2. Good.

Now, let's think about if a cell value1 could appear on the diagonal between a farther 1 and turn cell? If it did, the sequence would have a 1 at a non-start position, invalid. But the path values are determined by grid; if there is a 1 in between, then the path wouldn't be valid because the expected value at that position is 2 or0, not1. So pre recurrence would fail at that cell, preventing extension. Thus no issue.

Now, let's consider if `straight` for a cell value2 could be computed through a cell value1? No, because next expected from 0 is2, from2 is0, never1. So straight won't include 1 except as start. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `grid[i+1][j+1] == nxt[grid[i][j]]` in straight; if neighbor value matches, we add straight[neighbor]. But what if straight[neighbor] includes a turn? No, straight is no-turn. Good.

Now, let's consider if we should compute straight for all directions before pre. Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a more memory-efficient way to store straight as 4 matrices but pre as one. We'll do that.

Now, let's think about if `straight = [[[1] * m for _ in range(n)] for _ in range(4)]` might be slow due to list multiplication? Fine.

Now, let's consider if we can avoid storing `straight` for direction d when computing pre for d? We need straight[cw(d)]. Since cw(d) cycles through all directions. We need all. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can compute straight and pre in one pass per direction to reduce memory? Not necessary.

- straight[DL][2][4] =? Direction DL (1,-1). Process rows descending. At i=4, all1. i=3, j=2? Wait turn cell (2,4) row2. For row2, neighbor (3,3). grid[2][4]=0, nxt[0]=2, grid[3][3]=2 -> straight[DL][2][4] = 1 + straight[DL][3][3]. At row3, neighbor (4,2). grid[3][3]=2, nxt[2]=0, grid[4][2]=0 -> straight[DL][3][3] = 1 + straight[DL][4][2] =2. So straight[DL][2][4]=3. Good.
- pre DR at (2,4): direction DR predecessor (1,3). Process rows ascending. row0: (0,2)=1 pre=1. row1: (1,3)=2, predecessor (0,2) value1, nxt[1]=2, pre[0][2]=1 -> pre=2. row2: (2,4)=0, predecessor (1,3) value2, nxt[2]=0, pre[1][3]=2 -> pre=3. ans combine with straight[DL][2][4]=3 =>5. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the order of directions in `dirs` and `straight` matrices. We must ensure `straight[d]` corresponds to `dirs[d]`. We'll define:
```
dirs = [(1,1), (1,-1), (-1,-1), (-1,1)]
```
Then d=0 DR, d=1 DL, d=2 UL, d=3 UR. cw=(d+1)%4. Good.

Now, let's think about if in pre loops, `di,dj = dirs[d]` and predecessor `pi = i - di`, `pj = j - dj`. For d=0, di=1,dj=1 => p=(i-1,j-1). Good. For d=1, di=1,dj=-1 => p=(i-1,j+1). Good. For d=2, di=-1,dj=-1 => p=(i+1,j+1). Good. For d=3, di=-1,dj=1 => p=(i+1,j-1). Good.

Now, let's think about if in straight loops, we use neighbor `i+di, j+dj` implicitly by direction-specific conditions. Could also use dirs to generalize:
```
for d in range(4):
    di,dj=dirs[d]
    if di == 1: rows = range(n-1,-1,-1) else rows = range(n)
    for i in rows:
        for j in range(m):
            ni = i + di
            nj = j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and grid[ni][nj] == nxt[grid[i][j]]:
                straight[d][i][j] = straight[d][ni][nj] + 1
```
This is simpler and less error-prone! Since row order can be determined by di: if di==1, neighbor row i+1, process rows descending; if di==-1, process rows ascending. Column order arbitrary. Let's use this generalized loop for straight. It will be cleaner.

Similarly pre:
```
for d in range(4):
    di,dj = dirs[d]
    pre = [[0]*m for _ in range(n)]
    if di == 1: rows = range(n)  # predecessor row i-1, process ascending
    else: rows = range(n-1, -1, -1)  # predecessor row i+1, process descending

        for j in range(m):
            ...
            pi = i - di
            pj = j - dj
```
This is concise. Need ensure for straight, if di==1, neighbor row i+1, process rows descending. If di==-1, neighbor row i-1, process rows ascending. Good. For d=3 di=-1, neighbor row i-1, process ascending; neighbor col j+1 in row i-1 processed. Good.

This generalized code is easier and less bug-prone. Let's adopt.

Straight computation:
```
straight = [[[1] * m for _ in range(n)] for _ in range(4)]
for d in range(4):
    di, dj = dirs[d]
    if di == 1:
        rows = range(n-1, -1, -1)
    else:
        rows = range(n)
    mat = straight[d]
    for i in rows:
        row = mat[i]
        grid_row = grid[i]
        ni_base = i + di
        for j in range(m):
            ni = ni_base
            nj = j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and grid[ni][nj] == nxt[grid_row[j]]:
                row[j] = mat[ni][nj] + 1
```
Need note `ni_base` constant for row. `nj = j + dj`. Good.

Pre computation:
```
for d in range(4):
    di, dj = dirs[d]
    pre = [[0] * m for _ in range(n)]
    if di == 1:
        rows = range(n)
    else:
        rows = range(n-1, -1, -1)
    d2 = (d + 1) % 4
    straight2 = straight[d2]
    for i in rows:
        pre_row = pre[i]
        grid_row = grid[i]
        pi_base = i - di
        for j in range(m):
            if grid_row[j] == 1:
                pre_row[j] = 1
            else:
                pi = pi_base
                pj = j - dj
                if 0 <= pi < n and 0 <= pj < m:
                    if grid_row[j] == nxt[grid[pi][pj]] and pre[pi][pj] > 0:
                        pre_row[j] = pre[pi][pj] + 1
            if pre_row[j]:
                ...
```
Need be careful: `pi_base = i - di`; for di=1, pi=i-1; for di=-1, pi=i+1. Good.

Now, let's think about if `grid_row[j] == nxt[grid[pi][pj]]` and `pre[pi][pj] > 0`; if `pre[pi][pj]` is 0, no extension. But what if `grid_row[j] == 1` and `pre[pi][pj] >0`? We handle grid_row[j]==1 first, so pre=1. But as argued, condition false anyway. Good.

if pre_row[j] > ans:

```

Makes at most one clockwise 90-degree turn to another diagonal direction while maintaining the sequence."

Could the segment "starts with 1" mean the first element of the segment is 1, but the segment could be a subpath of a larger diagonal line and not necessarily start at a cell with no valid predecessor? We start at any 1. Good.

Potential code:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def lenOfVDiagonal(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(grid)
        m = len(grid[0])
        dirs = [(1,1), (1,-1), (-1,-1), (-1,1)]
        nxt = [2, 2, 0]  # next value after 0,1,2

        straight = [[[1] * m for _ in range(n)] for _ in range(4)]

        for d in range(4):
            di, dj = dirs[d]
            mat = straight[d]
            if di == 1:
                rows = range(n-1, -1, -1)
            else:
                rows = range(n)
            for i in rows:
                row = mat[i]
                grid_row = grid[i]
                ni = i + di
                for j in range(m):
                    nj = j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and grid[ni][nj] == nxt[grid_row[j]]:
                        row[j] = mat[ni][nj] + 1

        ans = 0
        for d in range(4):
            di, dj = dirs[d]
            pre = [[0] * m for _ in range(n)]
            if di == 1:
                rows = range(n)
            else:
                rows = range(n-1, -1, -1)
            d2 = (d + 1) % 4
            straight2 = straight[d2]
            for i in rows:
                pre_row = pre[i]
                grid_row = grid[i]
                pi = i - di
                for j in range(m):
                    if grid_row[j] == 1:
                        pre_row[j] = 1
                    else:
                        pj = j - dj
                        if 0 <= pi < n and 0 <= pj < m:
                            if grid_row[j] == nxt[grid[pi][pj]] and pre[pi][pj] > 0:
                                pre_row[j] = pre[pi][pj] + 1
                    if pre_row[j] > 0:
                        val = pre_row[j] + straight2[i][j] - 1
                        if val > ans:
                            ans = val
        return ans
```
